My heart starts pounding so hard that I have to grip onto something or step outside just to catch my breath.\n\nDoctor: Are these intense physical reactions and the urge to escape primarily triggered by the specific situation that reminds you of the collapse, or do you experience them in response to a broader range of stimuli or even when there's no obvious reminder of the event?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a mix of both. Seeing things like old warehouses or exposed beams instantly triggers a tight chest and this overwhelming urge to escape or avoid them. But it also happens out of nowhere when I'm just sitting at my desk, and suddenly my heart is pounding and I'm hearing the collapse all over again.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you experience these flashbacks or memories of the collapse when you're not in a situation that directly reminds you of it, and do these memories feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes, they happen completely out of nowhere, and it absolutely feels like it's happening all over again in that moment. I'll suddenly hear my coworker screaming and see the mezzanine crashing down in my mind, making my heart pound so hard that I have to grip my desk or step outside just to breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these flashbacks or intense reactions are exclusively related to the collapse event, or are there other situations, thoughts, or stimuli that can trigger similar intense anxiety or fear responses in you?\n\nPatient: They are definitely all tied back to that collapse and anything that reminds me of it. It's triggered by seeing exposed beams, driving past old warehouses, or even hearing minor creaking sounds at home. Any of those things will instantly make me feel like everything is about to collapse again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or places beyond just those that directly remind you of the collapse, such as social gatherings or daily routines, due to fear of triggering these intense reactions?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been avoiding social gatherings or my daily routines because of fear. My avoidance is almost entirely focused on those work-related structures and old buildings, though I have noticed I'm withdrawing from my husband lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge, easily startled, or constantly looking out for potential dangers, even when you're not in situations that remind you of the collapse?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and easily startled, even at home where I lie awake wired until 3 a.m. and jolt awake at the slightest creak thinking the house is collapsing. I'm also on such a short fuse lately that I'll snap at my husband over nothing and just feel too wound up to ever calm down.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by being 'on edge' and 'easily startled', such as how you react to sudden noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: I am constantly on edge, especially at night when I just lie there wired for hours. If I do finally fall asleep, the slightest noise like a creak in the house makes me jolt awake instantly, terrified that something is collapsing. It feels like my body is always anticipating another disaster, so I can never truly relax."

It has gotten to the point where I just freeze up and can't do my work, and I really need some help.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you're in those situations, do you experience any physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or difficulty breathing?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Whenever I'm near those buildings or when the memories suddenly rush back, my chest tightens up so much that it's really hard to breathe. My heart starts pounding so hard that I have to grip onto something or step outside just to catch my breath.\n\nDoctor: Are these intense physical reactions and the urge to escape primarily triggered by the specific situation that reminds you of the collapse, or do you experience them in response to a broader range of stimuli or even when there's no obvious reminder of the event?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a mix of both. Seeing things like old warehouses or exposed beams instantly triggers a tight chest and this overwhelming urge to escape or avoid them. But it also happens out of nowhere when I'm just sitting at my desk, and suddenly my heart is pounding and I'm hearing the collapse all over again.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you experience these flashbacks or memories of the collapse when you're not in a situation that directly reminds you of it, and do these memories feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes, they happen completely out of nowhere, and it absolutely feels like it's happening all over again in that moment. I'll suddenly hear my coworker screaming and see the mezzanine crashing down in my mind, making my heart pound so hard that I have to grip my desk or step outside just to breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these flashbacks or intense reactions are exclusively related to the collapse event, or are there other situations, thoughts, or stimuli that can trigger similar intense anxiety or fear responses in you?\n\nPatient: They are definitely all tied back to that collapse and anything that reminds me of it. It's triggered by seeing exposed beams, driving past old warehouses, or even hearing minor creaking sounds at home. Any of those things will instantly make me feel like everything is about to collapse again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or places beyond just those that directly remind you of the collapse, such as social gatherings or daily routines, due to fear of triggering these intense reactions?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been avoiding social gatherings or my daily routines because of fear. My avoidance is almost entirely focused on those work-related structures and old buildings, though I have noticed I'm withdrawing from my husband lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge, easily startled, or constantly looking out for potential dangers, even when you're not in situations that remind you of the collapse?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and easily startled, even at home where I lie awake wired until 3 a.m. and jolt awake at the slightest creak thinking the house is collapsing. I'm also on such a short fuse lately that I'll snap at my husband over nothing and just feel too wound up to ever calm down.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by being 'on edge' and 'easily startled', such as how you react to sudden noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: I am constantly on edge, especially at night when I just lie there wired for hours. If I do finally fall asleep, the slightest noise like a creak in the house makes me jolt awake instantly, terrified that something is collapsing. It feels like my body is always anticipating another disaster, so I can never truly relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions since the collapse, or do you find that your mind is preoccupied with thoughts of safety and potential dangers most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, my concentration has been terrible, and I take much longer to complete my inspection reports because I'm constantly second-guessing my decisions. My mind is completely preoccupied with safety concerns and worst-case scenarios, leaving me with this constant, heavy feeling that everything around me is just waiting to collapse."

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Since that collapse on the job, I can’t bring myself to go into certain buildings or walk under unfinished structures without feeling like I have to get out immediately. It has gotten to the point where I just freeze up and can't do my work, and I really need some help.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you're in those situations, do you experience any physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or difficulty breathing?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Whenever I'm near those buildings or when the memories suddenly rush back, my chest tightens up so much that it's really hard to breathe. My heart starts pounding so hard that I have to grip onto something or step outside just to catch my breath.\n\nDoctor: Are these intense physical reactions and the urge to escape primarily triggered by the specific situation that reminds you of the collapse, or do you experience them in response to a broader range of stimuli or even when there's no obvious reminder of the event?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a mix of both. Seeing things like old warehouses or exposed beams instantly triggers a tight chest and this overwhelming urge to escape or avoid them. But it also happens out of nowhere when I'm just sitting at my desk, and suddenly my heart is pounding and I'm hearing the collapse all over again.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you experience these flashbacks or memories of the collapse when you're not in a situation that directly reminds you of it, and do these memories feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes, they happen completely out of nowhere, and it absolutely feels like it's happening all over again in that moment. I'll suddenly hear my coworker screaming and see the mezzanine crashing down in my mind, making my heart pound so hard that I have to grip my desk or step outside just to breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these flashbacks or intense reactions are exclusively related to the collapse event, or are there other situations, thoughts, or stimuli that can trigger similar intense anxiety or fear responses in you?\n\nPatient: They are definitely all tied back to that collapse and anything that reminds me of it. It's triggered by seeing exposed beams, driving past old warehouses, or even hearing minor creaking sounds at home. Any of those things will instantly make me feel like everything is about to collapse again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or places beyond just those that directly remind you of the collapse, such as social gatherings or daily routines, due to fear of triggering these intense reactions?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been avoiding social gatherings or my daily routines because of fear. My avoidance is almost entirely focused on those work-related structures and old buildings, though I have noticed I'm withdrawing from my husband lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge, easily startled, or constantly looking out for potential dangers, even when you're not in situations that remind you of the collapse?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and easily startled, even at home where I lie awake wired until 3 a.m. and jolt awake at the slightest creak thinking the house is collapsing. I'm also on such a short fuse lately that I'll snap at my husband over nothing and just feel too wound up to ever calm down.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by being 'on edge' and 'easily startled', such as how you react to sudden noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: I am constantly on edge, especially at night when I just lie there wired for hours. If I do finally fall asleep, the slightest noise like a creak in the house makes me jolt awake instantly, terrified that something is collapsing. It feels like my body is always anticipating another disaster, so I can never truly relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions since the collapse, or do you find that your mind is preoccupied with thoughts of safety and potential dangers most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, my concentration has been terrible, and I take much longer to complete my inspection reports because I'm constantly second-guessing my decisions. My mind is completely preoccupied with safety concerns and worst-case scenarios, leaving me with this constant, heavy feeling that everything around me is just waiting to collapse.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned avoiding certain buildings or structures due to fear, but have you also been avoiding talking or thinking about the collapse itself, or is it something that you find yourself constantly dwelling on?\n\nPatient: I can't avoid thinking about it even if I try, because the memory constantly forces its way into my mind. I find myself constantly reliving the screaming and seeing the falling mezzanine, and I lie awake at night replaying the events over and over while blaming myself for what happened.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned blaming yourself for what happened during the collapse, can you tell me more about what specifically you blame yourself for and how that guilt affects you?"

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Since that collapse on the job, I can’t bring myself to go into certain buildings or walk under unfinished structures without feeling like I have to get out immediately. It has gotten to the point where I just freeze up and can't do my work, and I really need some help.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you're in those situations, do you experience any physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or difficulty breathing?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Whenever I'm near those buildings or when the memories suddenly rush back, my chest tightens up so much that it's really hard to breathe. My heart starts pounding so hard that I have to grip onto something or step outside just to catch my breath.\n\nDoctor: Are these intense physical reactions and the urge to escape primarily triggered by the specific situation that reminds you of the collapse, or do you experience them in response to a broader range of stimuli or even when there's no obvious reminder of the event?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a mix of both. Seeing things like old warehouses or exposed beams instantly triggers a tight chest and this overwhelming urge to escape or avoid them. But it also happens out of nowhere when I'm just sitting at my desk, and suddenly my heart is pounding and I'm hearing the collapse all over again.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you experience these flashbacks or memories of the collapse when you're not in a situation that directly reminds you of it, and do these memories feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes, they happen completely out of nowhere, and it absolutely feels like it's happening all over again in that moment. I'll suddenly hear my coworker screaming and see the mezzanine crashing down in my mind, making my heart pound so hard that I have to grip my desk or step outside just to breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these flashbacks or intense reactions are exclusively related to the collapse event, or are there other situations, thoughts, or stimuli that can trigger similar intense anxiety or fear responses in you?\n\nPatient: They are definitely all tied back to that collapse and anything that reminds me of it. It's triggered by seeing exposed beams, driving past old warehouses, or even hearing minor creaking sounds at home. Any of those things will instantly make me feel like everything is about to collapse again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or places beyond just those that directly remind you of the collapse, such as social gatherings or daily routines, due to fear of triggering these intense reactions?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been avoiding social gatherings or my daily routines because of fear. My avoidance is almost entirely focused on those work-related structures and old buildings, though I have noticed I'm withdrawing from my husband lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge, easily startled, or constantly looking out for potential dangers, even when you're not in situations that remind you of the collapse?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and easily startled, even at home where I lie awake wired until 3 a.m. and jolt awake at the slightest creak thinking the house is collapsing. I'm also on such a short fuse lately that I'll snap at my husband over nothing and just feel too wound up to ever calm down.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by being 'on edge' and 'easily startled', such as how you react to sudden noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: I am constantly on edge, especially at night when I just lie there wired for hours. If I do finally fall asleep, the slightest noise like a creak in the house makes me jolt awake instantly, terrified that something is collapsing. It feels like my body is always anticipating another disaster, so I can never truly relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions since the collapse, or do you find that your mind is preoccupied with thoughts of safety and potential dangers most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, my concentration has been terrible, and I take much longer to complete my inspection reports because I'm constantly second-guessing my decisions. My mind is completely preoccupied with safety concerns and worst-case scenarios, leaving me with this constant, heavy feeling that everything around me is just waiting to collapse.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned avoiding certain buildings or structures due to fear, but have you also been avoiding talking or thinking about the collapse itself, or is it something that you find yourself constantly dwelling on?\n\nPatient: I can't avoid thinking about it even if I try, because the memory constantly forces its way into my mind. I find myself constantly reliving the screaming and seeing the falling mezzanine, and I lie awake at night replaying the events over and over while blaming myself for what happened.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned blaming yourself for what happened during the collapse, can you tell me more about what specifically you blame yourself for and how that guilt affects you?\n\nPatient: After the collapse, investigators heavily scrutinized my reports and decisions, which made me feel entirely responsible for what happened. I can't shake the belief that I'm a failure who put people in danger, and I constantly beat myself up for not somehow preventing it. This guilt just sits in my gut like a heavy, constant dread every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your guilt and self-blame over the collapse are as distressing to you as the flashbacks and avoidance of certain buildings, or are they a separate issue that you're struggling with?"
  }

When she finally froze during a follow-up inspection at a similar warehouse and had to leave the site, her supervisor, concerned about both her safety and performance, recommended she speak with Occupational Health, which led to a referral for psychiatric evaluation.\n- Chief Complaint: “Since that collapse on the job, I can’t bring myself to go into certain buildings or walk under unfinished structures without feeling like I have to get out immediately.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie there wired until two or three in the morning, goes off. \n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere I’ll suddenly hear that guy screaming and see the mezzanine coming down in my mind like it’s happening all over again, and my heart starts pounding so hard I have to grip the edge of my desk or step outside just to catch my breath. \n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): If I even drive past a big old warehouse or a building with exposed beams, my to fall apart on you. \n- S074 (Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State): Most of the time I’m walking around with this heavy mix of dread and guilt in my gut, either terrified something bad is about to happen or beating myself up for not somehow preventing what happened at that site. \n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I’m on such a short fuse now that my husband can just ask how my day went and I’ll snap at him or slam a cabinet door, then immediately feel awful but still be too wound up to calm down. \n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the way home from work I’ve started driving way over the speed limit or weaving through traffic without really caring, almost like I’m daring something to happen because part of me feels like I don’t deserve to be safe after what happened. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve had to turn down or hand off multiple inspections, missed a follow-up visit after freezing on-site, and my supervisor has warned me about my performance, while at home my anger and withdrawal are causing more arguments with my husband and we barely spend time together anymore.\n- duration: These problems have been going on steadily since a few weeks after the warehouse collapse, so for about four months now.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint.     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Since that collapse on the job, I can’t bring myself to go into certain buildings or walk under unfinished structures without feeling like I have to get out immediately. It has gotten to the point where I just freeze up and can't do my work, and I really need some help.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you're in those situations, do you experience any physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or difficulty breathing?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Whenever I'm near those buildings or when the memories suddenly rush back, my chest tightens up so much that it's really hard to breathe. My heart starts pounding so hard that I have to grip onto something or step outside just to catch my breath.\n\nDoctor: Are these intense physical reactions and the urge to escape primarily triggered by the specific situation that reminds you of the collapse, or do you experience them in response to a broader range of stimuli or even when there's no obvious reminder of the event?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a mix of both. Seeing things like old warehouses or exposed beams instantly triggers a tight chest and this overwhelming urge to escape or avoid them. But it also happens out of nowhere when I'm just sitting at my desk, and suddenly my heart is pounding and I'm hearing the collapse all over again.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you experience these flashbacks or memories of the collapse when you're not in a situation that directly reminds you of it, and do these memories feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes, they happen completely out of nowhere, and it absolutely feels like it's happening all over again in that moment. I'll suddenly hear my coworker screaming and see the mezzanine crashing down in my mind, making my heart pound so hard that I have to grip my desk or step outside just to breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these flashbacks or intense reactions are exclusively related to the collapse event, or are there other situations, thoughts, or stimuli that can trigger similar intense anxiety or fear responses in you?\n\nPatient: They are definitely all tied back to that collapse and anything that reminds me of it. It's triggered by seeing exposed beams, driving past old warehouses, or even hearing minor creaking sounds at home. Any of those things will instantly make me feel like everything is about to collapse again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or places beyond just those that directly remind you of the collapse, such as social gatherings or daily routines, due to fear of triggering these intense reactions?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been avoiding social gatherings or my daily routines because of fear. My avoidance is almost entirely focused on those work-related structures and old buildings, though I have noticed I'm withdrawing from my husband lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge, easily startled, or constantly looking out for potential dangers, even when you're not in situations that remind you of the collapse?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and easily startled, even at home where I lie awake wired until 3 a.m. and jolt awake at the slightest creak thinking the house is collapsing. I'm also on such a short fuse lately that I'll snap at my husband over nothing and just feel too wound up to ever calm down.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by being 'on edge' and 'easily startled', such as how you react to sudden noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: I am constantly on edge, especially at night when I just lie there wired for hours. If I do finally fall asleep, the slightest noise like a creak in the house makes me jolt awake instantly, terrified that something is collapsing. It feels like my body is always anticipating another disaster, so I can never truly relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions since the collapse, or do you find that your mind is preoccupied with thoughts of safety and potential dangers most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, my concentration has been terrible, and I take much longer to complete my inspection reports because I'm constantly second-guessing my decisions. My mind is completely preoccupied with safety concerns and worst-case scenarios, leaving me with this constant, heavy feeling that everything around me is just waiting to collapse.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned avoiding certain buildings or structures due to fear, but have you also been avoiding talking or thinking about the collapse itself, or is it something that you find yourself constantly dwelling on?\n\nPatient: I can't avoid thinking about it even if I try, because the memory constantly forces its way into my mind. I find myself constantly reliving the screaming and seeing the falling mezzanine, and I lie awake at night replaying the events over and over while blaming myself for what happened.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned blaming yourself for what happened during the collapse, can you tell me more about what specifically you blame yourself for and how that guilt affects you?\n\nPatient: After the collapse, investigators heavily scrutinized my reports and decisions, which made me feel entirely responsible for what happened. I can't shake the belief that I'm a failure who put people in danger, and I constantly beat myself up for not somehow preventing it. This guilt just sits in my gut like a heavy, constant dread every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your guilt and self-blame over the collapse are as distressing to you as the flashbacks and avoidance of certain buildings, or are they a separate issue that you're struggling with?\n\nPatient: They are deeply connected and honestly equally exhausting. The belief that I'm a failure who put people in danger feeds directly into my panic, making the flashbacks feel like a constant punishment. It's like the guilt makes me feel like I deserve the terror and deserve to be kept out of those buildings.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of worthlessness or suicidal ideation due to the overwhelming guilt and self-blame you've been carrying since the collapse?"
  }

She was pinned briefly by debris, heard a coworker screaming, and watched as emergency crews extracted an injured laborer with serious crush injuries; subsequent investigations and legal reviews heavily scrutinized her reports and decisions, leaving her feeling simultaneously blamed and terrified by how close she had come to being killed. In the months following the incident, her previously confident, meticulous approach to inspections deteriorated—she began avoiding certain types of sites, taking excessive time to complete reports, and turning down travel assignments, while at home her husband noticed she was increasingly tense, withdrawn, and preoccupied with worst-case scenarios. When she finally froze during a follow-up inspection at a similar warehouse and had to leave the site, her supervisor, concerned about both her safety and performance, recommended she speak with Occupational Health, which led to a referral for psychiatric evaluation.\n- Chief Complaint: “Since that collapse on the job, I can’t bring myself to go into certain buildings or walk under unfinished structures without feeling like I have to get out immediately.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie there wired until two or three in the morning, replaying every inspection in my head, and when I do finally fall asleep I jolt awake at the slightest creak in the house convinced something’s collapsing again, so I never feel rested when the alarm goes off. \n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere I’ll suddenly hear that guy screaming and see the mezzanine coming down in my mind like it’s happening all over again, and my heart starts pounding so hard I have to grip the edge of my desk or step outside just to catch my breath. \n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): If I even drive past a big old warehouse or a building with exposed beams, my chest tightens and I’ll make up excuses to take a different route or hand off those jobs to someone else because I feel like if I walk under that structure it’s going to crush me. \n- S072 (Negative_Beliefs): Since the accident I can’t shake this belief that I’m a failure who put people in danger, and that no matter how careful I am, buildings—and the world in general—are just waiting to fall apart on you. \n- S074 (Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State): Most of the time I’m walking around with this heavy mix of dread and guilt in my gut, either terrified something bad is about to happen or beating myself up for not somehow preventing what happened at that site. \n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I’m on such a short fuse now that my husband can just ask how my day went and I’ll snap at him or slam a cabinet door, then immediately feel awful but still be too wound up to calm down. \n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the way home from work I’ve started driving way over the speed limit or weaving through traffic without really caring, almost like I’m daring something to happen because part of me feels like I don’t deserve to be safe after what happened. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve had to turn down or hand off multiple inspections, missed a follow-up visit after freezing on-site, and my supervisor has warned me about my performance, while at home my anger and withdrawal are causing more arguments with my husband and we barely spend time together anymore.\n- duration: These problems have been going on steadily since a few weeks after the warehouse collapse, so for about four months now.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Since that collapse on the job, I can’t bring myself to go into certain buildings or walk under unfinished structures without feeling like I have to get out immediately. It has gotten to the point where I just freeze up and can't do my work, and I really need some help.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you're in those situations, do you experience any physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or difficulty breathing?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Whenever I'm near those buildings or when the memories suddenly rush back, my chest tightens up so much that it's really hard to breathe. My heart starts pounding so hard that I have to grip onto something or step outside just to catch my breath.\n\nDoctor: Are these intense physical reactions and the urge to escape primarily triggered by the specific situation that reminds you of the collapse, or do you experience them in response to a broader range of stimuli or even when there's no obvious reminder of the event?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a mix of both. Seeing things like old warehouses or exposed beams instantly triggers a tight chest and this overwhelming urge to escape or avoid them. But it also happens out of nowhere when I'm just sitting at my desk, and suddenly my heart is pounding and I'm hearing the collapse all over again.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you experience these flashbacks or memories of the collapse when you're not in a situation that directly reminds you of it, and do these memories feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes, they happen completely out of nowhere, and it absolutely feels like it's happening all over again in that moment. I'll suddenly hear my coworker screaming and see the mezzanine crashing down in my mind, making my heart pound so hard that I have to grip my desk or step outside just to breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these flashbacks or intense reactions are exclusively related to the collapse event, or are there other situations, thoughts, or stimuli that can trigger similar intense anxiety or fear responses in you?\n\nPatient: They are definitely all tied back to that collapse and anything that reminds me of it. It's triggered by seeing exposed beams, driving past old warehouses, or even hearing minor creaking sounds at home. Any of those things will instantly make me feel like everything is about to collapse again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or places beyond just those that directly remind you of the collapse, such as social gatherings or daily routines, due to fear of triggering these intense reactions?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been avoiding social gatherings or my daily routines because of fear. My avoidance is almost entirely focused on those work-related structures and old buildings, though I have noticed I'm withdrawing from my husband lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge, easily startled, or constantly looking out for potential dangers, even when you're not in situations that remind you of the collapse?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and easily startled, even at home where I lie awake wired until 3 a.m. and jolt awake at the slightest creak thinking the house is collapsing. I'm also on such a short fuse lately that I'll snap at my husband over nothing and just feel too wound up to ever calm down.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by being 'on edge' and 'easily startled', such as how you react to sudden noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: I am constantly on edge, especially at night when I just lie there wired for hours. If I do finally fall asleep, the slightest noise like a creak in the house makes me jolt awake instantly, terrified that something is collapsing. It feels like my body is always anticipating another disaster, so I can never truly relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions since the collapse, or do you find that your mind is preoccupied with thoughts of safety and potential dangers most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, my concentration has been terrible, and I take much longer to complete my inspection reports because I'm constantly second-guessing my decisions. My mind is completely preoccupied with safety concerns and worst-case scenarios, leaving me with this constant, heavy feeling that everything around me is just waiting to collapse.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned avoiding certain buildings or structures due to fear, but have you also been avoiding talking or thinking about the collapse itself, or is it something that you find yourself constantly dwelling on?\n\nPatient: I can't avoid thinking about it even if I try, because the memory constantly forces its way into my mind. I find myself constantly reliving the screaming and seeing the falling mezzanine, and I lie awake at night replaying the events over and over while blaming myself for what happened.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned blaming yourself for what happened during the collapse, can you tell me more about what specifically you blame yourself for and how that guilt affects you?\n\nPatient: After the collapse, investigators heavily scrutinized my reports and decisions, which made me feel entirely responsible for what happened. I can't shake the belief that I'm a failure who put people in danger, and I constantly beat myself up for not somehow preventing it. This guilt just sits in my gut like a heavy, constant dread every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your guilt and self-blame over the collapse are as distressing to you as the flashbacks and avoidance of certain buildings, or are they a separate issue that you're struggling with?\n\nPatient: They are deeply connected and honestly equally exhausting. The belief that I'm a failure who put people in danger feeds directly into my panic, making the flashbacks feel like a constant punishment. It's like the guilt makes me feel like I deserve the terror and deserve to be kept out of those buildings.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of worthlessness or suicidal ideation due to the overwhelming guilt and self-blame you've been carrying since the collapse?\n\nPatient: I feel like a complete failure who doesn't deserve to be safe, which has actually led me to drive recklessly on my way home, almost daring something to happen to me. But while I have those self-destructive impulses and feel like I don't care what happens, I don't have any active plans or explicit thoughts of suicide.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your feelings of guilt and self-blame have affected your daily life and relationships, beyond just the avoidance of certain buildings or situations?"
  }
]

At home, it has severely strained my marriage because I've become so withdrawn and irritable, constantly snapping at my husband over the smallest things which leads to frequent arguments.

She was pinned briefly by debris, heard a coworker screaming, and watched as emergency crews extracted an injured laborer with serious crush injuries; subsequent investigations and legal reviews heavily scrutinized her reports and decisions, leaving her feeling simultaneously blamed and terrified by how close she had come to being killed. In the months following the incident, her previously confident, meticulous approach to inspections deteriorated—she began avoiding certain types of sites, taking excessive time to complete reports, and turning down travel assignments, while at home her husband noticed she was increasingly tense, withdrawn, and preoccupied with worst-case scenarios. When she finally froze during a follow-up inspection at a similar warehouse and had to leave the site, her supervisor, concerned about both her safety and performance, recommended she speak with Occupational Health, which led to a referral for psychiatric evaluation.\n- Chief Complaint: “Since that collapse on the job, I can’t bring myself to go into certain buildings or walk under unfinished structures without feeling like I have to get out immediately.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie there wired until two or three in the morning, replaying every inspection in my head, and when I do finally fall asleep I jolt awake at the slightest creak in the house convinced something’s collapsing again, so I never feel rested when the alarm goes off. \n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere I’ll suddenly hear that guy screaming and see the mezzanine coming down in my mind like it’s happening all over again, and my heart starts pounding so hard I have to grip the edge of my desk or step outside just to catch my breath. \n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): If I even drive past a big old warehouse or a building with exposed beams, my chest tightens and I’ll make up excuses to take a different route or hand off those jobs to someone else because I feel like if I walk under that structure it’s going to crush me. \n- S072 (Negative_Beliefs): Since the accident I can’t shake this belief that I’m a failure who put people in danger, and that no matter how careful I am, buildings—and the world in general—are just waiting to fall apart on you. \n- S074 (Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State): Most of the time I’m walking around with this heavy mix of dread and guilt in my gut, either terrified something bad is about to happen or beating myself up for not somehow preventing what happened at that site. \n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I’m on such a short fuse now that my husband can just ask how my day went and I’ll snap at him or slam a cabinet door, then immediately feel awful but still be too wound up to calm down. \n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the way home from work I’ve started driving way over the speed limit or weaving through traffic without really caring, almost like I’m daring something to happen because part of me feels like I don’t deserve to be safe after what happened. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve had to turn down or hand off multiple inspections, missed a follow-up visit after freezing on-site, and my supervisor has warned me about my performance, while at home my anger and withdrawal are causing more arguments with my husband and we barely spend time together anymore.\n- duration: These problems have been going on steadily since a few weeks after the warehouse collapse, so for about four months now.\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list She was pinned briefly by debris, heard a coworker screaming, and watched as emergency crews extracted an injured laborer with serious crush injuries; subsequent investigations and legal reviews heavily scrutinized her reports and decisions, leaving her feeling simultaneously blamed and terrified by how close she had come to being killed. In the months following the incident, her previously confident, meticulous approach to inspections deteriorated—she began avoiding certain types of sites, taking excessive time to complete reports, and turning down travel assignments, while at home her husband noticed she was increasingly tense, withdrawn, and preoccupied with worst-case scenarios. When she finally froze during a follow-up inspection at a similar warehouse and had to leave the site, her supervisor, concerned about both her safety and performance, recommended she speak with Occupational Health, which led to a referral for psychiatric evaluation.\n- Chief Complaint: “Since that collapse on the job, I can’t bring myself to go into certain buildings or walk under unfinished structures without feeling like I have to get out immediately.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie there wired until two or three in the morning, replaying every inspection in my head, and when I do finally fall asleep I jolt awake at the slightest creak in the house convinced something’s collapsing again, so I never feel rested when the alarm goes off. \n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere I’ll suddenly hear that guy screaming and see the mezzanine coming down in my mind like it’s happening all over again, and my heart starts pounding so hard I have to grip the edge of my desk or step outside just to catch my breath. \n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): If I even drive past a big old warehouse or a building with exposed beams, my chest tightens and I’ll make up excuses to take a different route or hand off those jobs to someone else because I feel like if I walk under that structure it’s going to crush me. \n- S072 (Negative_Beliefs): Since the accident I can’t shake this belief that I’m a failure who put people in danger, and that no matter how careful I am, buildings—and the world in general—are just waiting to fall apart on you. \n- S074 (Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State): Most of the time I’m walking around with this heavy mix of dread and guilt in my gut, either terrified something bad is about to happen or beating myself up for not somehow preventing what happened at that site. \n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I’m on such a short fuse now that my husband can just ask how my day went and I’ll snap at him or slam a cabinet door, then immediately feel awful but still be too wound up to calm down. \n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the way home from work I’ve started driving way over the speed limit or weaving through traffic without really caring, almost like I’m daring something to happen because part of me feels like I don’t deserve to be safe after what happened. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve had to turn down or hand off multiple inspections, missed a follow-up visit after freezing on-site, and my supervisor has warned me about my performance, while at home my anger and withdrawal are causing more arguments with my husband and we barely spend time together anymore.\n- duration: These problems have been going on steadily since a few weeks after the warehouse collapse, so for about four months now.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “Since that collapse on the job, I can’t bring myself to go into certain buildings or walk under unfinished structures without feeling like I have to get out immediately.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Clarify that while you feel you don't deserve to be safe, you don't have an active plan to harm yourself or end your life."

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Since that collapse on the job, I can’t bring myself to go into certain buildings or walk under unfinished structures without feeling like I have to get out immediately. It has gotten to the point where I just freeze up and can't do my work, and I really need some help.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you're in those situations, do you experience any physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or difficulty breathing?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Whenever I'm near those buildings or when the memories suddenly rush back, my chest tightens up so much that it's really hard to breathe. My heart starts pounding so hard that I have to grip onto something or step outside just to catch my breath.\n\nDoctor: Are these intense physical reactions and the urge to escape primarily triggered by the specific situation that reminds you of the collapse, or do you experience them in response to a broader range of stimuli or even when there's no obvious reminder of the event?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a mix of both. Seeing things like old warehouses or exposed beams instantly triggers a tight chest and this overwhelming urge to escape or avoid them. But it also happens out of nowhere when I'm just sitting at my desk, and suddenly my heart is pounding and I'm hearing the collapse all over again.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you experience these flashbacks or memories of the collapse when you're not in a situation that directly reminds you of it, and do these memories feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes, they happen completely out of nowhere, and it absolutely feels like it's happening all over again in that moment. I'll suddenly hear my coworker screaming and see the mezzanine crashing down in my mind, making my heart pound so hard that I have to grip my desk or step outside just to breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these flashbacks or intense reactions are exclusively related to the collapse event, or are there other situations, thoughts, or stimuli that can trigger similar intense anxiety or fear responses in you?\n\nPatient: They are definitely all tied back to that collapse and anything that reminds me of it. It's triggered by seeing exposed beams, driving past old warehouses, or even hearing minor creaking sounds at home. Any of those things will instantly make me feel like everything is about to collapse again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or places beyond just those that directly remind you of the collapse, such as social gatherings or daily routines, due to fear of triggering these intense reactions?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been avoiding social gatherings or my daily routines because of fear. My avoidance is almost entirely focused on those work-related structures and old buildings, though I have noticed I'm withdrawing from my husband lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge, easily startled, or constantly looking out for potential dangers, even when you're not in situations that remind you of the collapse?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and easily startled, even at home where I lie awake wired until 3 a.m. and jolt awake at the slightest creak thinking the house is collapsing. I'm also on such a short fuse lately that I'll snap at my husband over nothing and just feel too wound up to ever calm down.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by being 'on edge' and 'easily startled', such as how you react to sudden noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: I am constantly on edge, especially at night when I just lie there wired for hours. If I do finally fall asleep, the slightest noise like a creak in the house makes me jolt awake instantly, terrified that something is collapsing. It feels like my body is always anticipating another disaster, so I can never truly relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions since the collapse, or do you find that your mind is preoccupied with thoughts of safety and potential dangers most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, my concentration has been terrible, and I take much longer to complete my inspection reports because I'm constantly second-guessing my decisions. My mind is completely preoccupied with safety concerns and worst-case scenarios, leaving me with this constant, heavy feeling that everything around me is just waiting to collapse.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned avoiding certain buildings or structures due to fear, but have you also been avoiding talking or thinking about the collapse itself, or is it something that you find yourself constantly dwelling on?\n\nPatient: I can't avoid thinking about it even if I try, because the memory constantly forces its way into my mind. I find myself constantly reliving the screaming and seeing the falling mezzanine, and I lie awake at night replaying the events over and over while blaming myself for what happened.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned blaming yourself for what happened during the collapse, can you tell me more about what specifically you blame yourself for and how that guilt affects you?\n\nPatient: After the collapse, investigators heavily scrutinized my reports and decisions, which made me feel entirely responsible for what happened. I can't shake the belief that I'm a failure who put people in danger, and I constantly beat myself up for not somehow preventing it. This guilt just sits in my gut like a heavy, constant dread every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your guilt and self-blame over the collapse are as distressing to you as the flashbacks and avoidance of certain buildings, or are they a separate issue that you're struggling with?\n\nPatient: They are deeply connected and honestly equally exhausting. The belief that I'm a failure who put people in danger feeds directly into my panic, making the flashbacks feel like a constant punishment. It's like the guilt makes me feel like I deserve the terror and deserve to be kept out of those buildings.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of worthlessness or suicidal ideation due to the overwhelming guilt and self-blame you've been carrying since the collapse?\n\nPatient: I feel like a complete failure who doesn't deserve to be safe, which has actually led me to drive recklessly on my way home, almost daring something to happen to me. But while I have those self-destructive impulses and feel like I don't care what happens, I don't have any active plans or explicit thoughts of suicide.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your feelings of guilt and self-blame have affected your daily life and relationships, beyond just the avoidance of certain buildings or situations?\n\nPatient: This constant guilt and feeling like a failure has completely drained my emotional energy, making me feel like I don't deserve to be safe or successful at work anymore. At home, it has severely strained my marriage because I've become so withdrawn and irritable, constantly snapping at my husband over the smallest things which leads to frequent arguments.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having self-destructive impulses, can you tell me more about what you mean by 'daring something to happen' to you, and have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive?"
  }
]

On my drive home from work, I've started speeding and weaving through traffic without caring about safety, almost like I'm daring a crash to happen because of my guilt. While I feel like I don't deserve to be safe after surviving, I don't have any active plans to harm myself or end my life.

I find myself constantly reliving the screaming and seeing the falling mezzanine, and I lie awake at night replaying the events over and over while blaming myself for what happened.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned blaming yourself for what happened during the collapse, can you tell me more about what specifically you blame yourself for and how that guilt affects you?\n\nPatient: After the collapse, investigators heavily scrutinized my reports and decisions, which made me feel entirely responsible for what happened. I can't shake the belief that I'm a failure who put people in danger, and I constantly beat myself up for not somehow preventing it. This guilt just sits in my gut like a heavy, constant dread every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your guilt and self-blame over the collapse are as distressing to you as the flashbacks and avoidance of certain buildings, or are they a separate issue that you're struggling with?\n\nPatient: They are deeply connected and honestly equally exhausting. The belief that I'm a failure who put people in danger feeds directly into my panic, making the flashbacks feel like a constant punishment. It's like the guilt makes me feel like I deserve the terror and deserve to be kept out of those buildings.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of worthlessness or suicidal ideation due to the overwhelming guilt and self-blame you've been carrying since the collapse?\n\nPatient: I feel like a complete failure who doesn't deserve to be safe, which has actually led me to drive recklessly on my way home, almost daring something to happen to me. But while I have those self-destructive impulses and feel like I don't care what happens, I don't have any active plans or explicit thoughts of suicide.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your feelings of guilt and self-blame have affected your daily life and relationships, beyond just the avoidance of certain buildings or situations?\n\nPatient: This constant guilt and feeling like a failure has completely drained my emotional energy, making me feel like I don't deserve to be safe or successful at work anymore. At home, it has severely strained my marriage because I've become so withdrawn and irritable, constantly snapping at my husband over the smallest things which leads to frequent arguments.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having self-destructive impulses, can you tell me more about what you mean by 'daring something to happen' to you, and have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: On my drive home from work, I've started speeding and weaving through traffic without caring about safety, almost like I'm daring a crash to happen because of my guilt. While I feel like I don't deserve to be safe after surviving, I don't have any active plans to harm myself or end my life."
  }
]

    "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"F43.1\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Since that collapse on the job, I can’t bring myself to go into certain buildings or walk under unfinished structures without feeling like I have to get out immediately. It has gotten to the point where I just freeze up and can't do my work, and I really need some help.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you're in those situations, do you experience any physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or difficulty breathing?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Whenever I'm near those buildings or when the memories suddenly rush back, my chest tightens up so much that it's really hard to breathe. My heart starts pounding so hard that I have to grip onto something or step outside just to catch my breath.\n\nDoctor: Are these intense physical reactions and the urge to escape primarily triggered by the specific situation that reminds you of the collapse, or do you experience them in response to a broader range of stimuli or even when there's no obvious reminder of the event?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a mix of both. Seeing things like old warehouses or exposed beams instantly triggers a tight chest and this overwhelming urge to escape or avoid them. But it also happens out of nowhere when I'm just sitting at my desk, and suddenly my heart is pounding and I'm hearing the collapse all over again.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you experience these flashbacks or memories of the collapse when you're not in a situation that directly reminds you of it, and do these memories feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes, they happen completely out of nowhere, and it absolutely feels like it's happening all over again in that moment. I'll suddenly hear my coworker screaming and see the mezzanine crashing down in my mind, making my heart pound so hard that I have to grip my desk or step outside just to breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these flashbacks or intense reactions are exclusively related to the collapse event, or are there other situations, thoughts, or stimuli that can trigger similar intense anxiety or fear responses in you?\n\nPatient: They are definitely all tied back to that collapse and anything that reminds me of it. It's triggered by seeing exposed beams, driving past old warehouses, or even hearing minor creaking sounds at home. Any of those things will instantly make me feel like everything is about to collapse again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or places beyond just those that directly remind you of the collapse, such as social gatherings or daily routines, due to fear of triggering these intense reactions?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been avoiding social gatherings or my daily routines because of fear. My avoidance is almost entirely focused on those work-related structures and old buildings, though I have noticed I'm withdrawing from my husband lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge, easily startled, or constantly looking out for potential dangers, even when you're not in situations that remind you of the collapse?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and easily startled, even at home where I lie awake wired until 3 a.m. and jolt awake at the slightest creak thinking the house is collapsing. I'm also on such a short fuse lately that I'll snap at my husband over nothing and just feel too wound up to ever calm down.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by being 'on edge' and 'easily startled', such as how you react to sudden noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: I am constantly on edge, especially at night when I just lie there wired for hours. If I do finally fall asleep, the slightest noise like a creak in the house makes me jolt awake instantly, terrified that something is collapsing. It feels like my body is always anticipating another disaster, so I can never truly relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions since the collapse, or do you find that your mind is preoccupied with thoughts of safety and potential dangers most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, my concentration has been terrible, and I take much longer to complete my inspection reports because I'm constantly second-guessing my decisions. My mind is completely preoccupied with safety concerns and worst-case scenarios, leaving me with this constant, heavy feeling that everything around me is just waiting to collapse.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned avoiding certain buildings or structures due to fear, but have you also been avoiding talking or thinking about the collapse itself, or is it something that you find yourself constantly dwelling on?\n\nPatient: I can't avoid thinking about it even if I try, because the memory constantly forces its way into my mind. I find myself constantly reliving the screaming and seeing the falling mezzanine, and I lie awake at night replaying the events over and over while blaming myself for what happened.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned blaming yourself for what happened during the collapse, can you tell me more about what specifically you blame yourself for and how that guilt affects you?\n\nPatient: After the collapse, investigators heavily scrutinized my reports and decisions, which made me feel entirely responsible for what happened. I can't shake the belief that I'm a failure who put people in danger, and I constantly beat myself up for not somehow preventing it. This guilt just sits in my gut like a heavy, constant dread every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your guilt and self-blame over the collapse are as distressing to you as the flashbacks and avoidance of certain buildings, or are they a separate issue that you're struggling with?\n\nPatient: They are deeply connected and honestly equally exhausting. The belief that I'm a failure who put people in danger feeds directly into my panic, making the flashbacks feel like a constant punishment. It's like the guilt makes me feel like I deserve the terror and deserve to be kept out of those buildings.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of worthlessness or suicidal ideation due to the overwhelming guilt and self-blame you've been carrying since the collapse?\n\nPatient: I feel like a complete failure who doesn't deserve to be safe, which has actually led me to drive recklessly on my way home, almost daring something to happen to me. But while I have those self-destructive impulses and feel like I don't care what happens, I don't have any active plans or explicit thoughts of suicide.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your feelings of guilt and self-blame have affected your daily life and relationships, beyond just the avoidance of certain buildings or situations?\n\nPatient: This constant guilt and feeling like a failure has completely drained my emotional energy, making me feel like I don't deserve to be safe or successful at work anymore. At home, it has severely strained my marriage because I've become so withdrawn and irritable, constantly snapping at my husband over the smallest things which leads to frequent arguments.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having self-destructive impulses, can you tell me more about what you mean by 'daring something to happen' to you, and have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: On my drive home from work, I've started speeding and weaving through traffic without caring about safety, almost like I'm daring a crash to happen because of my guilt. While I feel like I don't deserve to be safe after surviving, I don't have any active plans to harm myself or end my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your feelings of guilt and worthlessness are more related to the trauma of the event itself or to a broader pattern of negative self-thoughts and low self-esteem that you've experienced before the collapse?\n\nPatient: These feelings of guilt and feeling like a failure definitely started only after the collapse and the investigations that followed. Before the accident, I was actually very confident in my work and never struggled with negative self-thoughts or low self-esteem."
  }

This guilt just sits in my gut like a heavy, constant dread every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your guilt and self-blame over the collapse are as distressing to you as the flashbacks and avoidance of certain buildings, or are they a separate issue that you're struggling with?\n\nPatient: They are deeply connected and honestly equally exhausting. The belief that I'm a failure who put people in danger feeds directly into my panic, making the flashbacks feel like a constant punishment. It's like the guilt makes me feel like I deserve the terror and deserve to be kept out of those buildings.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of worthlessness or suicidal ideation due to the overwhelming guilt and self-blame you've been carrying since the collapse?\n\nPatient: I feel like a complete failure who doesn't deserve to be safe, which has actually led me to drive recklessly on my way home, almost daring something to happen to me. But while I have those self-destructive impulses and feel like I don't care what happens, I don't have any active plans or explicit thoughts of suicide.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your feelings of guilt and self-blame have affected your daily life and relationships, beyond just the avoidance of certain buildings or situations?\n\nPatient: This constant guilt and feeling like a failure has completely drained my emotional energy, making me feel like I don't deserve to be safe or successful at work anymore. At home, it has severely strained my marriage because I've become so withdrawn and irritable, constantly snapping at my husband over the smallest things which leads to frequent arguments.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having self-destructive impulses, can you tell me more about what you mean by 'daring something to happen' to you, and have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: On my drive home from work, I've started speeding and weaving through traffic without caring about safety, almost like I'm daring a crash to happen because of my guilt. While I feel like I don't deserve to be safe after surviving, I don't have any active plans to harm myself or end my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your feelings of guilt and worthlessness are more related to the trauma of the event itself or to a broader pattern of negative self-thoughts and low self-esteem that you've experienced before the collapse?\n\nPatient: These feelings of guilt and feeling like a failure definitely started only after the collapse and the investigations that followed. Before the accident, I was actually very confident in my work and never struggled with negative self-thoughts or low self-esteem.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered seeking forgiveness from others or yourself for the perceived role you played in the collapse, and if so, how do you think that might affect your current feelings of guilt and self-blame?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought about seeking forgiveness, to be honest. I'm just so consumed by the guilt of what happened that it feels impossible, and deep down, I don't really feel like I deserve to be forgiven anyway."
  }

I constantly beat myself up for not somehow preventing the collapse in the first place, while also carrying the heavy weight of feeling like a failure who put people in danger. The subsequent investigations scrutinizing my reports and decisions have only made that guilt feel so much worse.

I'll suddenly hear my coworker screaming and see the mezzanine crashing down in my mind, making my heart pound so hard that I have to grip my desk or step outside just to breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these flashbacks or intense reactions are exclusively related to the collapse event, or are there other situations, thoughts, or stimuli that can trigger similar intense anxiety or fear responses in you?\n\nPatient: They are definitely all tied back to that collapse and anything that reminds me of it. It's triggered by seeing exposed beams, driving past old warehouses, or even hearing minor creaking sounds at home. Any of those things will instantly make me feel like everything is about to collapse again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or places beyond just those that directly remind you of the collapse, such as social gatherings or daily routines, due to fear of triggering these intense reactions?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been avoiding social gatherings or my daily routines because of fear. My avoidance is almost entirely focused on those work-related structures and old buildings, though I have noticed I'm withdrawing from my husband lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge, easily startled, or constantly looking out for potential dangers, even when you're not in situations that remind you of the collapse?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and easily startled, even at home where I lie awake wired until 3 a.m. and jolt awake at the slightest creak thinking the house is collapsing. I'm also on such a short fuse lately that I'll snap at my husband over nothing and just feel too wound up to ever calm down.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by being 'on edge' and 'easily startled', such as how you react to sudden noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: I am constantly on edge, especially at night when I just lie there wired for hours. At home, it has severely strained my marriage because I've become so withdrawn and irritable, constantly snapping at my husband over the smallest things which leads to frequent arguments.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having self-destructive impulses, can you tell me more about what you mean by 'daring something to happen' to you, and have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: On my drive home from work, I've started speeding and weaving through traffic without caring about safety, almost like I'm daring a crash to happen because of my guilt. Before the accident, I was actually very confident in my work and never struggled with negative self-thoughts or low self-esteem.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered seeking forgiveness from others or yourself for the perceived role you played in the collapse, and if so, how do you think that might affect your current feelings of guilt and self-blame?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought about seeking forgiveness, to be honest. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.
